Regularity theory for sub-critical p p -parabolic systems with measurable coefficients
Verena Bögelein, Frank Duzaar, Ugo Gianazza, Naian Liao

Abstract
. A quantitative regularity theory is developed for weak solutions to the parabolic system ∂ t u − div ​ 𝗔 ​ ( x , t , D ​ u ) = 0 in ​ E T ⊂ ℝ N × ℝ , \partial_{t}u-\mathrm{div}\,{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}(x,t,Du)=0\quad\text{in }E_{T}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathbb{R}, which features the p p -Laplacian with measurable coefficients. We focus on the sub-critical range 1 < p ≤ 2 ​ N N + 2 1<p\leq\tfrac{2N}{N+2} and obtain two main results. Local boundedness: starting from an L 𝗿 L^{\bm{\mathsf{r}}} -control of u u with 𝗿 > N ⁡ ( 2 − p ) p {\bm{\mathsf{r}}}>\frac{N(2-p)}{p} , we derive sharp, scale-invariant L ∞ L^{\infty} -estimates. Higher integrability of the gradient: | D ​ u | |Du| self-improves from L loc p L^{p}_{\rm loc} to L loc p ⁡ ( 1 + ε ) L^{p(1+\varepsilon)}_{\mathrm{loc}} for some ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 depending only on the data. The same results still hold given proper source terms.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35B65, 35K67, 35K40, 35K55

1.
Introduction and results

Gehring’s lemma on higher integrability exemplifies a fundamental self-improving phenomenon in nonlinear analysis: a reverse Hölder inequality leads to an automatic gain in integrability, thereby upgrading
L^{p}
-bounds to

L^{p+\varepsilon}
-regularity. Such effects are closely related to other quantitative self-improvement principles, including those arising from
p
-Poincaré inequalities on metric measure spaces,

p
-capacity conditions, and
p
-thickness assumptions. In each of these settings, one observes a characteristic openness of the underlying regularity condition: the validity of an estimate at an endpoint exponent automatically implies its persistence in a neighborhood of that exponent.

In the parabolic setting, this phenomenon governs the higher integrability of spatial gradients of weak solutions to degenerate parabolic systems, a cornerstone for partial regularity and fine quantitative estimates. Since the breakthrough of Kinnunen–Lewis
[
15
]
in 2000, higher integrability for gradients of weak solutions to parabolic
p
-Laplace systems has been established in the
super-critical
regime
p>\tfrac{2N}{N+2}
.
The complementary
sub-critical
regime
1<p\leq\tfrac{2N}{N+2}
has remained completely open until now.
This article closes that gap by developing a quantitative regularity theory for weak solutions
u\colon E_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
to

(1.1)

\partial_{t}u-\operatorname{div}\,{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}(x,t,u,Du)\;=\;{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}-\operatorname{div}\!\big(|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p-2}{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}\big)\quad\text{in}\>E_{T},

with
E_{T}\equiv E\times(0,T]
,
E
open in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, and source terms
{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}\in L^{qp}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E_{T},\mathbb{R}^{kN})
and
{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}\in L^{qp^{\prime}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E_{T},\mathbb{R}^{k})
, in the range

1<p\leq p_{c}:=\frac{2N}{N+2},\qquad q>\frac{N+p}{p}.

The vector field
{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}\colon E_{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kN}\to\mathbb{R}^{kN}
is Carathéodory – measurable in
(x,t)
, continuous in
(u,\xi)
–
and satisfies the following: for structural constants
0<C_{o}\leq C_{1}
, a fixed parameter
\mu\in[0,1]
, a.e.
(x,t)\in E_{T}
and all
(u,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kN}
,

(1.2)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}(x,t,u,\xi)\cdot\xi\geq C_{o}\big(\mu^{2}+|\xi|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|\xi|^{2}\,,\\[6.0pt]
|{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}(x,t,u,\xi)|\leq C_{1}\big(\mu^{2}+|\xi|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|\xi|\,,\\[6.0pt]
\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}_{\alpha}(x,t,u,\xi)\cdot u\,\xi_{\alpha}\cdot u\geq 0,\\
\end{array}\right.

where
{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}=\big({\bm{\mathsf{A}}}_{1},\dots,{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}_{N}\big)

and
\xi=\big(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{N}\big)

with
{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}_{\alpha},\xi_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.
The quantity
\mu
measures the nondegeneracy
(
\mu=0
corresponds to the degenerate case). The first two inequalities in (
1.2
) are the standard
p
-structure conditions – coercivity and
p
-growth – in the gradient variable
\xi
. The third line encodes a quasi-diagonal (directional) monotonicity of the nonlinearity along
u
. The directional monotonicity assumption in (
1.2
) goes back, in the context of
L^{\infty}
-estimates for elliptic systems, to Meier
[
16
]
. See
[
2
, Appendix A]
for its significance. In the scalar case, the directional monotonicity condition is redundant:
it follows directly from the coercivity inequality in (
1.2
).

Two principal conclusions are obtained. First, local boundedness of solutions in the sub-critical regime can be estimated by the
L^{{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}}
-integrability of solutions for some suitable
{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}>2
and the size of the source terms
{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}
&
{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}
.
We postpone the rigorous definition of solutions and notation to §
2
, and proceed with the statement of the first main result.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
p\in\bigl(1,\tfrac{2N}{N+2}\bigr]
,
q>\tfrac{N+p}{p}
, and
{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}>2
be such that

(1.3)

\bm{\lambda}_{{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}}:=N(p-2)+p{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}>0.

Assume that
u\in L^{{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E_{T},\mathbb{R}^{k})
is a weak solution in
E_{T}
to (
1.1
) under (
1.2
), with
{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}\in L^{qp}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E_{T},\mathbb{R}^{kN})
and
{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}\in L^{qp^{\prime}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E_{T},\mathbb{R}^{k})
. Then, we have

|u|\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E_{T}).

Moreover, there exists a constant
C=C(N,p,C_{o},C_{1},q,{\bm{\mathsf{r}}})
such that for any cylinder
Q_{\varrho,\theta}\equiv B_{\varrho}(x_{o})\times(t_{o}-\theta,t_{o}]
in
E_{T}
and any
\tau\in\bigl(0,1\bigr)
,

\displaystyle\sup_{Q_{\tau\varrho,\tau\theta}}|u|

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg[\frac{1}{(1-\tau)^{N+p}}\Big(\frac{\varrho^{p}}{\theta}\Big)^{\frac{N}{p}}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-\!-$}}\kern-8.47218pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-5.86113pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-4.56949pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-3.81949pt}\!\iint_{Q_{\varrho,\theta}}|u|^{{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{p}{\bm{\lambda}_{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}}}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+C\bigg[\Big(\frac{\theta}{\varrho^{p}}\Big)^{q-\frac{N}{p}}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-\!-$}}\kern-8.47218pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-5.86113pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-4.56949pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-3.81949pt}\!\iint_{Q_{\varrho,\theta}}|\varrho\,{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{qp}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{p}{\bm{\lambda}_{2q}}}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+C\bigg[\Big(\frac{\theta}{\varrho^{p}}\Big)^{p^{\prime}(q-\frac{N}{p})}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-\!-$}}\kern-8.47218pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-5.86113pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-4.56949pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-3.81949pt}\!\iint_{Q_{\varrho,\theta}}|\varrho^{p}\,{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}|^{qp^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{p}{p^{\prime}\bm{\lambda}_{q}}}+C\Big(\frac{\theta}{\varrho^{p}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2-p}}+C\mu\varrho.

Remark 1.2
.

The constant
C(N,p,C_{o},C_{1},q,{\bm{\mathsf{r}}})\to\infty
either as
p\to 1
or as
q\to\frac{N+p}{p}
, and it stays stable as
p\uparrow\frac{2N}{N+2}
for any fixed
{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}>2
.
The situation becomes more delicate as
{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}\to\frac{N(2-p)}{p}
, that is, as
\bm{\lambda}_{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}\to 0
.
Indeed, on the one hand, when
\bm{\lambda}_{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}\to 0
, the iteration in the proof of Lemma
3.3
does not converge, and one cannot conclude – by this argument – that
u
is bounded, even in a purely qualitative sense. On the other hand, under the assumption
u\in L^{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}
, the final part of the proof of Theorem
1.1
starts from a qualitative

L^{\infty}
-bound and turns it into a quantitative one.
For technical reasons, two different ranges of
{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}

must be considered, namely either
2\leq\frac{N(2-p)}{p}<{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}<p^{\prime}+2
or
p^{\prime}+2\leq{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}
.
If we restrict our attention to the former case, the proof shows that the constant
C(N,p,C_{o},C_{1},q,{\bm{\mathsf{r}}})\to\infty

as
{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}\to\frac{N(2-p)}{p}
.
This phenomenon is related to the difficulty of obtaining a quantitative
L^{\infty}
-bound when
p=p_{c}
, as mentioned below in Remark
1.6
.

The set
\{N,\,p,\,C_{o},\,C_{1},\,q,\,{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}\}
is referred to as the
structural data
. The intrinsic geometry pertinent to system (
1.1
) will be reflected in the
L^{\infty}
-estimate upon choosing the size of
Q_{\varrho,\theta}
.
This will be crucial for our second result, that is, the gradient of solutions enjoys a Gehring–type self-improvement.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
N\geq 2
,
p\in\big(1,\tfrac{2N}{N+2}\big]
,
q>\tfrac{N+p}{p}
,
\mu=0
,
and let
{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}>2
be such that (
1.3
) is satisfied.
Moreover, assume in the vector-valued case (
k>1
) that the vector field
{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}
is independent of
u
, that is,
{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}={\bm{\mathsf{A}}}(x,t,\xi)
.
Then, there exists
\varepsilon_{o}=\varepsilon_{o}(N,p,C_{o},C_{1},q,{\bm{\mathsf{r}}})\in(0,1]

with the following property.
If
{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}\in L^{qp}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E_{T},\mathbb{R}^{kN})
,
{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}\in L^{qp^{\prime}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E_{T},\mathbb{R}^{kN})
,
and
u
is a weak solution in
E_{T}
to (
1.1
) under (
1.2
) satisfying

u\in L^{{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E_{T},\mathbb{R}^{k})
, then

|Du|\in L^{p(1+\varepsilon_{o})}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E_{T}).

Moreover, for every
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{o}]
and every cylinder

Q_{8R}\equiv B_{8R}(x_{o})\times(t_{o}-(8R)^{2},\,t_{o}+(8R)^{2})
in

E_{T}
,
the following quantitative higher–integrability estimate holds:

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-\!-$}}\kern-8.47218pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-5.86113pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-4.56949pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-3.81949pt}\!\iint_{Q_{R}}

\displaystyle|Du|^{p(1+\varepsilon)}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\,{\bm{\mathsf{H}}}(8R)^{p\varepsilon\frac{{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}}{2}}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-\!-$}}\kern-8.47218pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-5.86113pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-4.56949pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-3.81949pt}\!\iint_{Q_{2R}}|Du|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+C\bigg[\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-\!-$}}\kern-8.47218pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-5.86113pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-4.56949pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-3.81949pt}\!\iint_{Q_{2R}}\Big(|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{qp}+|R\,{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}|^{qp^{\prime}}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{1+\varepsilon}{q}},

where

{\bm{\mathsf{H}}}(8R):=1+\bigg[\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-\!-$}}\kern-8.47218pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-5.86113pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-4.56949pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-3.81949pt}\!\iint_{Q_{8R}}\frac{|u-(u)_{8R}|^{{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}}}{(8R)^{{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{2}{\bm{\lambda}_{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}}}+\bigg[\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-\!-$}}\kern-8.47218pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-5.86113pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-4.56949pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-3.81949pt}\!\iint_{Q_{8R}}\big(|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{qp}+|R\,{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}|^{qp^{\prime}}\big)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}}{q\bm{\lambda}_{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}}},

and
(u)_{8R}
denotes the average of
u
on
Q_{8R}
, and
C
depends only on
N,p,C_{o},C_{1}
,
q,{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}
.

Remark 1.4
.

In Theorem
1.3
we assume
\mu=0
mainly for simplicity, but a similar result could be proved also for
\mu\in(0,1]
.

Remark 1.5
.

Since the proof of Theorem
1.3
relies heavily on Theorem
1.1
, the stability of
\varepsilon_{o}
is strongly affected by the asymptotic behavior of the constant
C
, as discussed in Remark
1.2
. In particular, tracing the dependence of the constants and parameters on the structural data in the proof of Theorem
1.3
shows that
\varepsilon_{o}\to 0
not only as either
p\to 1
or
q\to\frac{N+p}{p}
, but also as
{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}\to\frac{N(2-p)}{p}
. Note for fixed
{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}>2
,
\varepsilon_{o}
remains positive as
p\uparrow\frac{2N}{N+2}
.

Remark 1.6
.

In the borderline case
p=p_{c}=\frac{2N}{N+2}
, the situation becomes particularly delicate.
For the scalar parabolic
p
-Laplace equation (
k=1
), it has been shown in
[
3
]
that weak solutions are locally bounded in a qualitative sense, although a quantitative
L^{\infty}
-estimate in terms of the
L^{2}
-norm is not known so far.
This result implies that, in the model case, the assumption
u\in L^{{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E_{T})
for some
{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}>2
is automatically fulfilled, and can therefore be omitted in Theorem
1.3
. It is furthermore plausible that the argument extends to more general parabolic
p
-Laplace systems of similar structure as in (
1.1
), although such an extension has not yet been established rigorously.

1.1.
Novelty and significance

The phenomenon known as
higher integrability
– that is, the automatic gain of summability for the gradient beyond what is prescribed by the natural energy space – has long been recognized as a cornerstone in the transition from quantitative
a priori
control to qualitative regularity.
Its conceptual roots trace back to Gehring’s fundamental work
[
8
]
, and subsequent developments by Meyers and Elcrat
[
17
]
extended the principle to general elliptic systems.
After later refinements resolving technical issues concerning localization and supports
[
21
,
10
]
, the method became a standard element of modern regularity theory (see also
[
11
, Ch. V, Thm. 2.1]
,
[
14
, § 6.4]
).
In the parabolic setting, a first coherent treatment was provided by Giaquinta and Struwe
[
12
]
, who handled quasi-linear systems with measurable coefficients.
A decisive step forward was achieved by Kinnunen and Lewis
[
15
]
, who proved higher integrability for wide classes of parabolic
p
-Laplace-type systems under the
super-critical
condition
p>\tfrac{2N}{N+2}
.

In contrast, in the
sub-critical
regime
1<p\leq\tfrac{2N}{N+2}
, solutions can display an untamed analytic behavior.
In fact, the natural scaling and compactness structures underlying the parabolic
p
-Laplacian deteriorate, and a comprehensive regularity theory has remained out of reach; see, for instance,
[
4
]
and the discussion in
[
6
, Appendices A–B]
. The present work identifies a natural class of weak solutions to parabolic
p
-Laplace systems in the sub-critical regime and establishes a unified regularity theory for them that parallels the one in the super-critical range.
Precisely, we establish a quantitative reverse Hölder framework that yields

|Du|\in L^{p(1+\varepsilon)}_{\mathrm{loc}},

for some
\varepsilon>0
depending solely on the structural data, and complement it by a sharp local
L^{\infty}
-estimate for
u
within the sub-critical regime.

To the best of our knowledge, the first
L^{\infty}
-estimates for parabolic systems of
p
–Laplace-type with a general structure comparable to that considered here were established in
[
13
]
. This extended earlier work of O’Leary
[
18
]
, which was restricted to the simpler scalar case. In particular, the directional monotonicity assumption (
1.2
)
3
appears already in these contributions.
Although the authors pursue a two–step argument reminiscent of ours, their analysis does not yield the explicit and fully quantitative bounds obtained in Theorem
1.1
; instead, they conclude only a qualitative boundedness statement.

Regarding the higher integrability of
Du
for parabolic systems of
p
–Laplace-type, the structure
{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}={\bm{\mathsf{A}}}(x,t,\xi)
has a tradition – see, for instance,
[
15
]
. From a technical point of view, the absence of
u
-dependence ensures that if
u
is a solution, then so is
u+
constant, a property required in Lemma
4.6
and in several subsequent arguments that depend critically upon it.
It remains unclear whether this restriction reflects an intrinsic structural necessity or merely a limitation of the present method.

1.2.
Sharpness of the result

Now, we demonstrate some examples that test the sharpness of our results – boundedness and higher integrability. Consider the prototype, scalar equation

(1.4)

\partial_{t}u-\Div\big(|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u\big)=0

in
\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathbb{R}
. We first discuss
boundedness
.
Unbounded weak solutions occur when
1<p<p_{c}:=\frac{2N}{N+2}
.
In particular, the function

(1.5)

\begin{array}[]{c}{\displaystyle u(x,t)=\left[|\lambda|\left(\frac{p}{2-p}\right)^{p-1}\right]^{\frac{1}{2-p}}\frac{(T-t)_{+}^{\frac{1}{2-p}}}{|x|^{\frac{p}{2-p}}}},\\
\\
{\displaystyle 1<p<\frac{2N}{N+2},\qquad\lambda=N(p-2)+p}\end{array}

is a nonnegative local weak solution to (
1.4
).
This solution is finite for
t<T
and
x\neq 0
, but blows up as
x\to 0
.
This example shows that the threshold
p>p_{c}
is almost optimal for boundedness.

A second counterexample to boundedness for
1<p<\tfrac{2N}{N+2}
can be constructed by seeking solutions to (
1.4
) of the self–similar form

(1.6)

u(x,t)=g\big(|x|(T-t)_{+}^{\beta}\big),

where
T\in\mathbb{R}
and the exponent
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
is to be determined.
Here
g
denotes a positive, radially symmetric, monotonically decreasing function, to be computed below.
If we assume
g
to be regular and smooth, it is straightforward to verify that
g=g(r)
must satisfy the ordinary differential equation

\frac{1}{p}rg^{\prime}+\frac{N-1}{r}(-g^{\prime})^{p-1}-(p-1)(-g^{\prime})^{p-2}g^{\prime\prime}=0.

Here the sign in front of
g^{\prime}
reflects the fact that
g^{\prime}\leq 0
, and
\beta=-\frac{1}{p}
. The equation can be re-written as

(1.7)

\frac{r}{p}-\frac{N-1}{r}(-g^{\prime})^{p-2}+(p-1)(-g^{\prime})^{p-3}g^{\prime\prime}=0,

and the substitution
\displaystyle y:=(-g^{\prime})^{p-2}
transforms (
1.7
) into the first–order linear ordinary differential equation

(1.8)

y^{\prime}=\frac{p-2}{p-1}\bigg[\frac{r}{p}-\frac{N-1}{r}y\bigg],

which admits an explicit solution. Since
1<p<\tfrac{2N}{N+2}
, a straightforward integration of (
1.8
) yields

y=r^{2}\left[C\,r^{\frac{|\lambda|}{p-1}}+\frac{2-p}{p|\lambda|}\right],

where
\lambda
is as above, and
C>0
is an arbitrary constant, taken positive for simplicity of presentation. Reverting to
g^{\prime}
, we obtain

g^{\prime}(r)=-\,r^{-\frac{2}{2-p}}\left[C\,r^{\frac{|\lambda|}{p-1}}+\frac{2-p}{p|\lambda|}\right]^{-\frac{1}{2-p}}.

This expression coincides with the formula given in
[
1
, § 1, (1.9)]
,
within the broader analysis of explicit and semi-explicit solutions to the parabolic
p
-Laplacian in the singular range
1<p<2
. We refrain from detailing the explicit computation of
g
; for our purposes it suffices to observe that

g(r)\simeq\bigg[\Big(\frac{p}{2-p}\Big)^{p-1}|\lambda|\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2-p}}r^{-\frac{p}{2-p}}\qquad\text{as}\>\>r\downarrow 0,

which in turn implies

u(x,t)\simeq\bigg[\Big(\frac{p}{2-p}\Big)^{p-1}|\lambda|\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2-p}}\left[\frac{(T-t)_{+}}{|x|^{p}}\right]^{\frac{1}{2-p}}\qquad\text{as}\>\>\frac{|x|}{(T-t)_{+}^{1/p}}\downarrow 0.

Notice that the behavior of
u
near the origin coincides exactly with that of (
1.5
).
The restriction
1<p<\tfrac{2N}{N+2}
ensures that both
u
and its spatial gradient possess
the integrability required for the natural weak formulation of the problem,
that is,
u
belongs to the energy class associated with parabolic
p
-Laplace equation, and therefore represents a genuine weak solution to (
1.4
) in the sense of
Definition
2.5
.

As explained in Remark
1.6
, when
p=\frac{2N}{N+2}
and for the scalar equation (
k=1
), solutions are locally bounded. However, it is unclear whether a quantitative estimate as stated in Theorem
1.1
can be achieved, or there exists a sequence of solutions that are uniformly bounded in the function space (
2.1
) but whose limit turns out to be essentially unbounded. Needless to say, one would like to understand the vector-valued case (
k>1
) as well.

We now turn to the question of
higher integrability
. As pointed out in Remark
1.6
, our approach actually shows, at least in the scalar equation case, that
\nabla u
admits a higher integrability estimate that is uniform for all
p\geq\frac{2N}{N+2}
. However, in general one cannot expect such a uniform higher integrality for
p\leq\frac{2N}{N+2}
with the mere notion of solution.
Indeed, a direct computation shows that the solution (
1.5
) satisfies

\nabla u\in L^{p+\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathbb{R})\quad\text{for all }\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{p}),\qquad\varepsilon_{p}\equiv-\frac{N(p-2)+2p}{2}>0,

and that
\varepsilon_{p}\downarrow 0
as
p\uparrow p_{c}
.

This example also hints that the issue of higher integrability is intimately connected to the issue of boundedness.
To illustrate this point,
suppose that
p\in(1,\frac{2N}{N+2}]
and
u
is a non-negative local weak solution to (
1.4
) satisfying
\nabla u\in L^{p+\varepsilon}_{\loc}(E_{T})
for some
a priori
determined
\varepsilon
with
\varepsilon\in[\varepsilon_{*},1]
for some fixed
\varepsilon_{*}>0
independent of
p
.
Then, by the parabolic embedding theorem, we have
u\in L^{{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}}_{\loc}(E_{T})
for
{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}=(p+\varepsilon)\frac{N+2}{N}
. By a straightforward algebraic computation, one finds a positive number

\delta=\delta(N,\varepsilon_{*})>0
such that, whenever

p\in(p_{c}-\delta,\;p_{c}]
,
the quantity
N(p-2)+p{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}
remains strictly positive.
Indeed, recalling the definition of
p_{c}
and using
p_{c}-\delta<p\leq p_{c}
, one readily computes

\displaystyle N(p-2)+p{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}

\displaystyle>N(p_{c}-2-\delta)+(p_{c}-\delta)(p_{c}-\delta+\varepsilon)\tfrac{N+2}{N}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{N(p_{c}-2)+2\tfrac{2N}{N+2}}_{=0}-N\delta-2\delta+2(\varepsilon-\delta)-\delta(\varepsilon-\delta)\tfrac{N+2}{N}

\displaystyle=-(N+4)\delta+2\varepsilon-\delta(\varepsilon-\delta)\tfrac{N+2}{N}.

To ensure that the last quantity is positive, it suffices, for instance, to take

\delta=\frac{\varepsilon_{*}N}{N(N+4)+N+2}.

Since, in this range of
p
, one also has
{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}>2
, it follows from Lemma
3.3
that such a solution
(for
p\in(p_{c}-\delta,\;p_{c}]
) must in fact be locally bounded in
E_{T}
. In principle this argument also applies to the systems as in (
1.1
), and justifies considering locally bounded solutions.

Second, consider equation (
1.4
) with a forcing term
{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}
.
Once again, we seek self-similar solutions to the form (
1.6
), with the specific choice
\beta=-\tfrac{1}{p}
.
In this setting, we prescribe

{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}(x,t)=\frac{1}{(T-t)_{+}}\,f\Big(|x|(T-t)_{+}^{-\frac{1}{p}}\Big),

where
f
is a given radial function.
Under these assumptions, a direct computation shows that the corresponding ordinary differential equation for
g
reads

\frac{r}{p}\,g^{\prime}(r)+\frac{N-1}{r}\,(-g^{\prime}(r))^{p-1}-(p-1)\,(-g^{\prime}(r))^{p-2}g^{\prime\prime}(r)=f(r).

If
p=\frac{2N}{N+2}
, and
f\colon(0,\tfrac{1}{2})\to\mathbb{R}
is given by

f(r)=r^{-\frac{N}{2}}(-\ln r)^{-\frac{N-2}{4}}\left[\frac{N}{2}+\frac{N^{2}-4N-4}{4N}(-\ln r)^{-1}\right],

then, the corresponding solution

u\colon B_{\frac{1}{2}}\times(0,T]\to\mathbb{R}

takes the form

u(x,t)=g\Big(|x|(T-t)_{+}^{-\frac{1}{p}}\Big),

where
g:(0,\tfrac{1}{2})\to\mathbb{R}
is a primitive of

g^{\prime}(r)=-r^{-\frac{N+2}{2}}(-\ln r)^{-\frac{N+2}{4}}.

Provided
N>4
, it can be verified that

u\in L^{p}\big(0,T;W^{1,p}(B_{\frac{1}{2}})\big)\quad\text{and}\quad{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}\in L^{2}\big(B_{\frac{1}{2}}\times(0,T)\big),

and that
u
is a weak solution to the parabolic
p
-Laplace equation with a source term

\partial_{t}u-\Div\big(|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u\big)={\bm{\mathsf{f}}}\quad\text{in }B_{\frac{1}{2}}\times(0,T).

At the same time,
\nabla u
fails to exhibit any form of higher integrability. The restriction
N>4

is essential to ensure that the source term

{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}
belongs to
L^{2}
.

1.3.
Strategy of the proof

From the examples discussed in the preceding subsection, it is apparent that the sub–critical regime exhibits substantially subtler behavior.
On the technical side, the main obstruction arises from the fact that the energy inequality (see §
4.1
), when combined with the parabolic Sobolev embedding, fails to provide any quantitative gain in the form of a reverse Hölder inequality.
More precisely, since
p\leq\frac{2N}{N+2}<2
, the dominant contribution on the right-hand side of the energy inequality comes from the
L^{2}
-term.
In light of the parabolic Sobolev embedding (Lemma
2.4
below), the space

L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{x}\cap L^{q}_{t}W^{1,q}_{x}

embeds into
L^{2}
only when
q\geq\tfrac{2N}{N+2}
, and hence
q\geq p
.
As a consequence, this standard approach cannot yield a reverse Hölder inequality in the sub–critical range.

This inherent difficulty is overcome by employing a quantified supremum bound for the solution.
The idea of using such bounds in the derivation of gradient estimates originates in
[
19
]
.
For this reason, we establish sharp quantitative sup-estimates in §
3.3
, which are of independent interest.
These estimates are, however, intrinsically non-homogeneous with respect to the solution.
Consequently, any subsequent argument that relies on these bounds, including the proof of the reverse Hölder inequality, inherits such a non-homogeneous dependence on
u
.

This necessitates the introduction of an intrinsic geometry that depends on the solution itself: all estimates are carried out on parabolic cylinders whose space-time scaling includes the magnitude of
u
.
At the same time, the underlying PDE is non-homogeneous with respect to the spatial gradient
Du
.
It has been known since the seminal works of DiBenedetto and Friedman
[
5
]
and of Kinnunen and Lewis
[
15
]
that, in this context, gradient estimates must be performed on cylinders intrinsic to
Du
.
This interplay between two distinct intrinsic
scalings – one involving
u
and the other its gradient
– creates a substantial technical obstacle: it precludes the use of cylinders intrinsic to a single quantity alone. In this sense, we operate within a framework of
mixed intrinsic scaling
:
our scaling parameter simultaneously controls the
L^{{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}}
-oscillation of
u
and is bounded in terms of the
L^{p}
-norm of
Du
and the source terms; see §
4.5
.
Within this setting, we establish a reverse Hölder inequality on the mixed intrinsic cylinders.
This, however, comes at the expense of a highly delicate construction:
the definition and selection of these cylinders are carried out in §§
5.1
–
5.3
.
The resulting structure yields a weak-type estimate for the super-level sets of
|Du|
,
which ultimately leads to the higher integrability of the spatial gradient.

2.
Notation, definitions and tools

Throughout the paper,
\mathbb{N}_{0}
denotes
\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, and
\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\mathbb{R}^{N}
are Euclidean spaces.
Given a bounded open set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
T>0
, we set
E_{T}:=E\times(0,T]
.
For a
scalar
function
v\colon E_{T}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\nabla v
denotes its spatial gradient;
for a
vector-valued
function
u\colon E_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
, we write
Du
for its spatial gradient.

A space–time point is denoted by
z_{o}=(x_{o},t_{o})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathbb{R}
, with
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
By
B_{\varrho}(x_{o})
we denote the open ball in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
centered at
x_{o}
with radius
\varrho>0
;
when
x_{o}=0
, we simply write
B_{\varrho}
. For
R,S>0
, we define the
backward parabolic cylinder

Q_{R,S}(z_{o}):=B_{R}(x_{o})\times(t_{o}-S,t_{o}].

Occasionally, we abbreviate
\sigma Q_{R,S}(z_{o}):=Q_{\sigma R,\sigma S}(z_{o})
for
\sigma>0
.
For
\lambda>0
and
z_{o}=(x_{o},t_{o})
, we introduce the
intrinsic parabolic cylinder

Q_{\varrho}^{(\lambda)}(z_{o}):=B_{\varrho}^{(\lambda)}(x_{o})\times\Lambda_{\varrho}(t_{o}),

where

B_{\varrho}^{(\lambda)}(x_{o}):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}:\ |x-x_{o}|<\lambda^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\varrho\},\qquad\Lambda_{\varrho}(t_{o}):=(t_{o}-\varrho^{2},t_{o}+\varrho^{2}).

When
\lambda=1
, the superscript is omitted, and when
z_{o}=(0,0)
or when the context is clear, the reference to
z_{o}
will also be dropped.

We record an elementary iteration lemma (cf.
[
14
, Lemma 6.1]
) that will be repeatedly used to absorb error terms along shrinking radii.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
0<\varepsilon<1
,
A,B\geq 0
and
\alpha\geq 0
. Then there exists a universal constant
C=C(\alpha,\varepsilon)

such that whenever
\phi\colon[R_{o},R_{1}]\to\mathbb{R}
is a non-negative bounded function satisfying

\phi(t)\leq\varepsilon\phi(s)+\frac{A}{(s-t)^{\alpha}}+B\qquad\text{for all $R_{o}\leq t<s\leq R_{1}$,}

we have

\phi(t)\leq C\,\bigg[\frac{A}{(s-t)^{\alpha}}+B\bigg]

for any
R_{o}\leq t<s\leq R_{1}
.

The following lemma
[
4
, Chapter I, Lemma 4.1]
provides a basic decay criterion for a nonlinear recurrence.
It asserts that, under a suitable smallness condition on the initial value, the sequence converges to zero at a geometric rate. Such statements are classical tools in iterative schemes, particularly in De Giorgi-type arguments for deriving

L^{\infty}
-bounds.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
(\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be a sequence of non-negative numbers satisfying

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{i+1}\leq C\,\bm{\mathsf{b}}^{i}\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{i}^{1+\alpha}\qquad\mbox{for all $i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}$}

for some positive constants
C,\alpha

and
\bm{\mathsf{b}}>1
.
If

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{o}\leq C^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\bm{\mathsf{b}}^{-\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}},

then
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{i}\to 0
as
i\to\infty
.

For a map
u\in L^{1}(0,T;L^{1}(E,\mathbb{R}^{k}))
and given measurable sets
A\subset E
and
G\subset E_{T}
with positive Lebesgue measure, the slicewise mean
\langle u\rangle_{A}\colon(0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
of
u
on
A
is defined
by

\langle u\rangle_{A}(t):=-\mskip-19.5mu\int_{A}u(t)\,\mathrm{d}x,\quad\mbox{for a.e.~$t\in(0,T)$,}

whereas the mean value
(u)_{G}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
of
u
on
G
is defined by

(u)_{G}:=\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-\!-$}}\kern-8.47218pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-5.86113pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-4.56949pt}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-\!-$}}\kern-3.81949pt}\!\iint_{G}u\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

Note that if
u\in C^{0}((0,T);L^{2}(E,\mathbb{R}^{k}))
, the slicewise means are defined for any
t\in(0,T)
.
If
A
is a ball
B_{\varrho}^{(\lambda)}(x_{o})
, we write
\langle u\rangle_{x_{o};\varrho}^{(\lambda)}(t):=\langle u\rangle_{B_{\varrho}^{(\lambda)}(x_{o})}(t)
. Similarly, if
G
is a
cylinder of the form
Q_{\varrho}^{(\lambda)}(z_{o})
, we use the shorthand
notation
(u)^{(\lambda)}_{z_{o};\varrho}:=(u)_{Q_{\varrho}^{(\lambda)}(z_{o})}
.

A convenient device repeatedly used below is the elementary quasi–minimality of the average
\mathbb{R}^{N}\ni a\mapsto\int_{A}|u-a|^{r}\,\mathrm{d}x
.
For completeness we record it.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
r\geq 1
,
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
be a bounded set of positive measure, and
u\in L^{r}(A,\mathbb{R}^{k})
. Then for every
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,

-\mskip-19.5mu\int_{A}\!|u-(u)_{A}|^{r}\,\mathrm{d}x\;\leq\;2^{r}-\mskip-19.5mu\int_{A}\!|u-a|^{r}\,\mathrm{d}x.

We shall repeatedly interpolate between the
L^{q}
-energy and
the
L^{r}
-mass of
u

by means of the following localized Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality on balls, stated in the precise form needed below.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty
. There exists a constant

c=c(N,p)
such that for any ball
B_{\varrho}(x_{o})\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
with
\varrho>0
and any function
u\in W^{1,p}(B_{\varrho}(x_{o}))
, we have

-\mskip-19.5mu\int_{B_{\varrho}(x_{o})}\frac{|u|^{q}}{\varrho^{q}}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq c\bigg[-\mskip-19.5mu\int_{B_{\varrho}(x_{o})}\frac{|u|^{2}}{\varrho^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg]^{\frac{q-p}{2}}-\mskip-19.5mu\int_{B_{\varrho}(x_{o})}\bigg[\frac{|u|^{p}}{\varrho^{p}}+|Du|^{p}\bigg]\mathrm{d}x,

where
q=p\frac{N+2}{N}
.

We end this section with the notion of solution.

Definition 2.5
.

Assume that the Carathéodory vector field

{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}\colon E_{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kN}\to\mathbb{R}^{kN}
satisfies (
1.2
), that

{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}\in L^{p}_{\rm loc}(E_{T},\mathbb{R}^{kN})
,
and that
{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}\in L^{2}_{\rm loc}(E_{T},\mathbb{R}^{k})
.
We identify a measurable map

u\colon E_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
in the class

(2.1)

u\in C\big([0,T];L^{2}(E,\mathbb{R}^{k})\big)\cap L^{p}\big(0,T;W^{1,p}(E,\mathbb{R}^{k})\big)

as a
weak solution
to the parabolic system (
1.1
) if and only if the identity

\displaystyle\iint_{E_{T}}\big[u\cdot\varphi_{t}-{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}(x,t,u,Du)\cdot D\varphi\big]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t=-\iint_{E_{T}}\big[|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p-2}{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}\cdot D\varphi+{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}\cdot\varphi\big]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

holds, for any testing function
\varphi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(E_{T},\mathbb{R}^{k})
.

\Box

3.
Local
L^{\infty}
-control in the sub-critical regime

We consider weak solutions to the quasilinear parabolic

p
-Laplace–type system (
1.1
) under structural conditions (
1.2
) and with the given source terms

{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}\colon E_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{kN}
,
{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}\colon E_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
. The precise regularity assumptions on
{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}
and
{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}
will be given in the following.

3.1.
Energy and Caccioppoli estimates

A weighted Caccioppoli estimate is required to run the Moser iteration.
The lemma below furnishes precisely this input: the Lipschitz weight
\Phi(|u|^{2})
and its primitive
v=\int_{0}^{|u|^{2}}\Phi(s)\,\mathrm{d}s
legitimize testing with powers of
u
against cut–offs and deliver the balance needed for the
L^{{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}}\!\to\!L^{\infty}
upgrade. This device is specific to Moser’s method; the De Giorgi approach will rely instead on unweighted truncation estimates.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
1<p\leq 2
and
\mu\in[0,1]
. There exists a constant
C=C(p,C_{o},C_{1})
with the following property. Suppose
u
is a weak solution to (
1.1
)
in
E_{T}
under the structural assumptions (
1.2
), and let
\Phi\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be non-negative, bounded, and Lipschitz continuous, with

(3.1)

C_{\Phi}:=\sup\Bigl\{\frac{s\,\Phi^{\prime}(s)}{\Phi(s)}:\ s\geq 0,\ \Phi(s)>0\Bigr\}<\infty.

If
Q_{R,S}(z_{o})\Subset E_{T}
and
\zeta\in C^{\infty}\big(Q_{R,S}(z_{o}),[0,1]\big)
vanishes on the parabolic boundary of
Q_{R,S}(z_{o})
, then

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau\in(t_{o}-S,t_{o}]}

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}(x_{o})\times\{\tau\}}\zeta^{p}v\,\mathrm{d}x+C_{o}\iint_{Q_{R,S}(z_{o})}\zeta^{p}|Du|^{p}\Phi\big(|u|^{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\iint_{Q_{R,S}(z_{o})}\Big[|u|^{p}|\nabla\zeta|^{p}+\mu^{p}\zeta^{p}\Big]\Phi(|u|^{2})\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

(3.2)

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+C\iint_{Q_{R,S}(z_{o})}v|\partial_{t}\zeta^{p}|\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+C\Big(1+C_{\Phi}^{\frac{p}{p-1}}\Big)\iint_{Q_{R,S}(z_{o})}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p}\Phi\big(|u|^{2}\big)\zeta^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+C\iint_{Q_{R,S}(z_{o})}|{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}||u|\Phi\big(|u|^{2}\big)\zeta^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

where

v:=\int_{0}^{|u|^{2}}\Phi(s)\,\mathrm{d}s.

Proof..

By translation, without loss of generality we may assume that
z_{o}=(0,0)
.
Fix
\tau\in(-S,0)
and consider
\delta\in(0,-\tau)
. Let us test the weak form of (
1.1
) with

\varphi(x,t):=u(x,t)\,\Phi\big(|u(x,t)|^{2}\big)\zeta(x,t)^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}(t),

where
\chi
is given by
\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\equiv 1
on
(-S,\tau)
,
\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\equiv 0
on
(\tau+\delta,0)
, and linear on
[\tau,\tau+\delta]
. A standard time–mollification argument justifies this choice of
\varphi
.
For the term containing the time derivative we compute, using integration by parts in
t
and

v:=\int_{0}^{|u|^{2}}\Phi(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,

that

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{R,S}}u\cdot\partial_{t}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\partial_{t}\big(|u|^{2}\big)\Phi\big(|u|^{2}\big)\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\partial_{t}v\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}v\partial_{t}(\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}})\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

To estimate the contribution of the diffusion term, we first differentiate the testing function. Using
D_{\alpha}|u|^{2}=2D_{\alpha}u\cdot u
, we obtain

\displaystyle D_{\alpha}\Big(u\Phi\big(|u|^{2}\big)\zeta^{p}\Big)

\displaystyle=D_{\alpha}u\Phi\big(|u|^{2}\big)\zeta^{p}+u\Phi^{\prime}\big(|u|^{2}\big)D_{\alpha}|u|^{2}\zeta^{p}+u\Phi\big(|u|^{2}\big)D_{\alpha}\zeta^{p}.

Inserting this into the weak formulation of (
1.1
), and recalling the definition of
v
, we arrive at

(3.3)

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}v\partial_{t}(\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}})\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}+{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}=-{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{3}+{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}+{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}

where

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}_{\alpha}(x,t,u,Du)\cdot D_{\alpha}u\Phi\big(|u|^{2}\big)\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}

\displaystyle=2\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}_{\alpha}(x,t,u,Du)\cdot uD_{\alpha}u\cdot u\Phi^{\prime}\big(|u|^{2}\big)\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{3}

\displaystyle=\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}_{\alpha}(x,t,u,Du)\cdot uD_{\alpha}\zeta^{p}\Phi\big(|u|^{2}\big)\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p-2}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}\cdot D_{\alpha}\big(u\Phi(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}\big)\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

and

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\iint_{Q_{R,S}}{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}\cdot u\Phi(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

Here, we recall that
\zeta\in C^{\infty}\big(Q_{R,S},[0,1]\big)
vanishes on the parabolic boundary of
Q_{R,S}
, and
\chi
is the time cut–off specified above. We begin with the diffusion part on the left-hand side, i.e.,
{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}+{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}
. By the ellipticity in (
1.2
)
1
and the directional monotonicity (
1.2
)
3
(together with
\Phi^{\prime}\geq 0
),

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}+{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}

\displaystyle\geq C_{o}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2})^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|Du|^{2}\Phi(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

Using the elementary identity

(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2})^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|Du|^{2}=(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2})^{\frac{p}{2}}-\mu^{2}(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2})^{\frac{p-2}{2}}

and the fact that for
1<p\leq 2
, we have
\mu^{2}(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2})^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\leq\mu^{p}
, we infer

{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}+{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}\geq C_{o}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2})^{\frac{p}{2}}\Phi(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t-C_{o}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\mu^{p}\Phi(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

Next we estimate the cut–off term
{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{3}
. Using the growth bound (
1.2
)
2
, we obtain

\displaystyle|{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{3}|

\displaystyle\leq\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}(x,t,u,Du)||u||\nabla\zeta^{p}|\Phi(|u|^{2})\,\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}p\iint_{Q_{R,S}}(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2})^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|Du||u||\nabla\zeta|\zeta^{p-1}\Phi(|u|^{2})\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}p\iint_{Q_{R,S}}(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2})^{\frac{p-1}{2}}|u||\nabla\zeta|\zeta^{p-1}\Phi(|u|^{2})\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

where in the last step we used
(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2})^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|Du|\leq(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2})^{\frac{p-1}{2}}
. Using Young’s inequality with a parameter
\varepsilon>0
, we have

C_{1}p(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2})^{\frac{p-1}{2}}|u||\nabla\zeta|\zeta^{p-1}\leq\varepsilon\,(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2})^{\frac{p}{2}}\zeta^{p}+(C_{1}p)^{p^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}|u|^{p}|\nabla\zeta|^{p}.

Substituting this into the bound for
{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{3}
obtained above yields

\displaystyle|{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{3}|

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\iint_{Q_{R,S}}(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2})^{\frac{p}{2}}\Phi(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+(C_{1}p)^{p^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|u|^{p}|\nabla\zeta|^{p}\Phi(|u|^{2})\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

where
\varepsilon>0
will be fixed later so that the first term can be absorbed into the left.
Finally, we control the contributions of the source terms
{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}
and
{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}
.
Using the product rule for

D_{\alpha}\big(u\Phi(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}\big)
,
the term in the weak formulation involving
-\Div(|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p-2}{\bm{\mathsf{F}}})
against the test function decomposes as

{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}=\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\big[{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{1}+{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{2}+{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{3}\big]\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

where

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p-2}{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}\cdot D_{\alpha}u\Phi(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p},

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{2}

\displaystyle:=p\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p-2}({\bm{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}\cdot u)\Phi(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p-1}D_{\alpha}\zeta,

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{3}

\displaystyle:=2\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p-2}({\bm{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}\cdot u)\,(D_{\alpha}u\cdot u)\Phi^{\prime}(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}.

The terms
{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{1}
and
{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{2}
are readily estimated by Young’s inequality, while
{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{3}
requires a separate treatment. In fact, let
p^{\prime}=\frac{p}{p-1}
. Since

|u|^{2}\Phi^{\prime}(|u|^{2})\leq\;C_{\Phi}\,\Phi(|u|^{2})

due to (
3.1
), we have

\displaystyle|{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{3}|

\displaystyle=2\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p-1}\Big(\frac{{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}}{|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|}\cdot u\Big)\big(D_{\alpha}u\cdot u\big)\Phi^{\prime}(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}

\displaystyle\leq 2|{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}|^{p-1}|Du||u|^{2}\Phi^{\prime}(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}\leq 2C_{\Phi}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p-1}\Phi(|u|^{2})|Du|\zeta^{p}.

Applying Young’s inequality with exponents
p
and
p^{\prime}
, and with
\varepsilon>0
gives

|{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{3}|\leq\varepsilon|Du|^{p}\Phi(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}+2^{p^{\prime}}C_{\Phi}^{p^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p}\Phi(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}.

Finally, replacing
|Du|^{p}
by
(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2})^{p/2}
yields the form consistent with the coercive term:

|{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{3}|\leq\varepsilon(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2})^{\frac{p}{2}}\Phi(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}+2^{p^{\prime}}C_{\Phi}^{p^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p}\Phi(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}.

For
{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{1}
we use
|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p-2}{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}\cdot D_{\alpha}u\leq|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p-1}|Du|
and Young’s inequality with exponents
p
and
p^{\prime}
and with a parameter
\varepsilon>0
, obtaining the pointwise bound

\displaystyle|{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{1}|

\displaystyle\leq|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p-1}|Du|\Phi(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2})^{\frac{p}{2}}\Phi(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}+\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p}\Phi(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}.

For
{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{2}
we observe that

|{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{2}|\leq p|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p-1}|u||\nabla\zeta|\,\zeta^{p-1}\Phi(|u|^{2}).

Then, Young’s inequality with exponents
p
and
p^{\prime}
gives

|{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{2}|\leq\varepsilon|u|^{p}|\nabla\zeta|^{p}\Phi(|u|^{2})+p^{p^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p}\,\Phi(|u|^{2})\,\zeta^{p}.

Combining the bounds for
{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{1}
,
{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{2}
, and
{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{3}
and substituting into the definition of
{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}
, we obtain, for every
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle|{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}|

\displaystyle\leq 2\varepsilon\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\Phi\big(|u|^{2}\big)\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\underbrace{\big(1+p^{p^{\prime}}+2^{p^{\prime}}C_{\Phi}^{p^{\prime}}\big)}_{\leq C(p)(1+C_{\Phi}^{p^{\prime}})}\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p}\Phi\big(|u|^{2}\big)\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\varepsilon\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|u|^{p}|\nabla\zeta|^{p}\Phi\big(|u|^{2}\big)\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

Combining the estimate for
{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}

with the bound for
{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}

obtained above and the trivial bound for
{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}
, we control the right–hand side of (
3.3
) by

\displaystyle|{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{3}|+|{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}|+|{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}|

\displaystyle\leq 3\varepsilon\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\Phi\big(|u|^{2}\big)\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\Big[(C_{1}p)^{p^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}+\varepsilon\Big]\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|u|^{p}|\nabla\zeta|^{p}\Phi(|u|^{2})\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+C(p)\big(1+C_{\Phi}^{p^{\prime}}\big)\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p}\Phi\big(|u|^{2}\big)\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}||u|\Phi(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

Combining the lower bound for
{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}+{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}

with the preceding estimate of
|{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}|+|{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}|+|{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}|

and rearranging terms in (
3.3
), we obtain

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}

\displaystyle v\partial_{t}(\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}})\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\big(C_{o}-3\varepsilon\big)\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\underbrace{(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2})^{\frac{p}{2}}}_{\geq|Du|^{p}}\Phi(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{o}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\mu^{p}\Phi(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\Big[(C_{1}p)^{p^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}+\varepsilon\Big]\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|u|^{p}|\nabla\zeta|^{p}\Phi(|u|^{2})\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+C(p)\big(1+C_{\Phi}^{p^{\prime}}\big)\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p}\Phi\big(|u|^{2}\big)\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}||u|\Phi(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

Fix
\varepsilon:=\frac{1}{6}C_{o}
.
Since
C_{o}-3\varepsilon=\frac{1}{2}C_{o}
, the previous inequality yields

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}

\displaystyle v\zeta^{p}\partial_{t}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\tfrac{1}{2}C_{o}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|Du|^{p}\Phi(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C(p)C_{o}\Big(\frac{C_{1}}{C_{o}}\Big)^{p^{\prime}}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\big[|u|^{p}|\nabla\zeta|^{p}+\mu^{p}\zeta^{p}\big]\Phi(|u|^{2})\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\frac{C(p)}{C_{o}^{\frac{1}{p-1}}}\big(1+C_{\Phi}^{p^{\prime}}\big)\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p}\Phi\big(|u|^{2}\big)\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}||u|\Phi(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\tfrac{1}{2}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}v|\partial_{t}\zeta^{p}|\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\Bigg[\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\big[|u|^{p}|\nabla\zeta|^{p}+\mu^{p}\zeta^{p}\big]\Phi(|u|^{2})\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\big(1+C_{\Phi}^{p^{\prime}}\big)\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p}\Phi\big(|u|^{2}\big)\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\Bigg]

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}||u|\Phi(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\tfrac{1}{2}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}v|\partial_{t}\zeta^{p}|\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

for a constant
C=C(p,C_{o}/C_{1})
(in particular, one may take
C\simeq C(p)C_{o}\,(C_{1}/C_{o})^{p^{\prime}}
, where
p^{\prime}=\tfrac{p}{p-1}
). We have used
C_{1}\geq 1
to streamline the constants.
Passing to the limit
\delta\downarrow 0
in the preceding inequality, we have
\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\downarrow\mathbf{1}_{(-S,\tau]}
and

\partial_{t}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\to-\delta_{t=\tau}
. The first integral on the left-hand side
converges to
\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{B_{R}\times\{\tau\}}v\zeta^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x
, while the second one can be estimated from below by
\iint_{B_{R}\times(-S,\tau]}|Du|^{p}\Phi(|u|^{2})\zeta^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t
. In the right-hand side integrals we bound the cut-off function
\chi
by 1. After that, we take the supremum over
\tau\in(-S,0]
in the first integral and let
\tau\uparrow 0
in the second one. In this way we get the desired energy estimate.
∎

A De Giorgi–type truncation estimate is needed to control super-level sets without resorting to weighted powers.
The next lemma provides this input: testing the system with the truncated vector
u(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\,\zeta^{p}
yields an intrinsic Caccioppoli inequality in which the gradient appears as
|\nabla|u||^{p}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}
. The parameter
{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\geq 0
localizes the energy to the set
\{|u|>{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\}
, the cut–off
\zeta
restricts to
Q_{R,S}(z_{o})
, and the nondegeneracy level
\mu
enters only through lower–order terms. This estimate is the quantitative engine behind the De Giorgi iteration: it converts measure decay of super-level sets into pointwise bounds while accommodating the source terms
{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}
and
{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}
under the structural hypotheses (
1.2
).
Note that it does not follow from Lemma
3.1
, since assumption (
3.1
) is not satisfied for the function
\Phi(s)=(s-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
1<p\leq 2
and
\mu\in[0,1]
. There exists a constant
C=C(p,C_{o},C_{1})
, such that whenever
u
is a locally bounded weak solution in
E_{T}
to (
1.1
)
with (
1.2
)
and
Q_{R,S}(z_{o})\subset E_{T}
, then for any cut-off function
\zeta\in C^{\infty}\big(Q_{R,S}(z_{o}),[0,1]\big)
that vanishes on the parabolic boundary of
Q_{R,S}(z_{o})
and any
{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau\in(t_{o}-S,t_{o}]}\int_{B_{R}(x_{o})\times\{\tau\}}v\zeta^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x+\iint_{Q_{R,S}(z_{o})}|\nabla|u||^{p}\big(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\big)_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C\iint_{Q_{R,S}(z_{o})}\Big[|u|^{p}|\nabla\zeta|^{p}+\mu^{p}\zeta^{p}\Big]\big(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\big)_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+C\iint_{Q_{R,S}(z_{o})}v|\partial_{t}\zeta^{p}|\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad\phantom{\leq\,}+C\iint_{Q_{R,S}(z_{o})}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p}|u|^{p^{\prime}}\mathbf{1}_{\{|u|>{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\}}\zeta^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\iint_{Q_{R,S}(z_{o})}|{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}||u|^{p^{\prime}+1}\mathbf{1}_{\{|u|>{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\}}\zeta^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

where

v:=\int_{0}^{|u|}s(s-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}s.

Proof..

By translation, we can always assume that
z_{o}=(0,0)
.
In the weak formulation of (
1.1
) we test with

\varphi(x,t):=u(x,t)\big(|u(x,t)|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\big)_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta(x,t)^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}(t),

where
p^{\prime}=\frac{p}{p-1}
is the Hölder conjugate exponent. Here,
\chi
is the time cut–off defined by
\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\equiv 1
on
(-S,\tau)
,
\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\equiv 0
on
(\tau+\delta,0)
, and linear on
[\tau,\tau+\delta]
.
Since
u
is locally bounded, this choice is admissible; a standard time–mollification (or Steklov averaging) justifies the use of
\varphi
. For the term involving the time derivative in the weak formulation, we compute

\displaystyle-\iint_{Q_{R,S}}u\cdot\partial_{t}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\partial_{t}|u||u|\big(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\big)_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\partial_{t}\bigg[\int_{0}^{|u|}s(s-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}s\bigg]\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=-\iint_{Q_{R,S}}v\partial_{t}(\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}})\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

To estimate the contribution of the diffusion term in the weak formulation, we first compute the spatial derivative of the testing function:

\displaystyle D_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\Big(u\big(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\big)_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}\Big)

\displaystyle=D_{\alpha}u\big(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\big)_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}+p^{\prime}u\big(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\big)_{+}^{p^{\prime}-1}\frac{u\cdot D_{\alpha}u}{|u|}\zeta^{p}+u\big(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\big)_{+}^{p^{\prime}}D_{\alpha}\zeta^{p}.

Together with the expression for the time derivative term, we arrive at the identity

(3.4)

-\iint_{Q_{R,S}}v\partial_{t}(\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}})\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}+{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}=-{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{3}+{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}+{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{2},

where we used the following abbreviations:

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}_{\alpha}(x,t,u,Du)\cdot D_{\alpha}u(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}

\displaystyle=p^{\prime}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}_{\alpha}(x,t,u,Du)\cdot u\frac{D_{\alpha}u\cdot u}{|u|}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}-1}\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{3}

\displaystyle=\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}_{\alpha}(x,t,u,Du)\cdot uD_{\alpha}\zeta^{p}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p-2}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}\cdot D_{\alpha}\left(u(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}\right)\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\iint_{Q_{R,S}}{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}\cdot u(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

Next, we consider the integral
{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}
on the left-hand side.
Using the coercivity assumption (
1.2
)
1
, we obtain

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}

\displaystyle\geq C_{o}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\left(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\right)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|Du|^{2}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=C_{o}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\left(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{=\,}-C_{o}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\underbrace{\left(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\right)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}}_{\leq\mu^{p-2}}\mu^{2}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq C_{o}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\left(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}-C_{o}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\mu^{p}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

Since
{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}\geq 0
due to (
1.2
)
3
, among the
{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}
-terms, it remains to estimate
{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{3}
from above. This can be done using the upper bound (
1.2
)
2
for
{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}
. Indeed, we obtain

\displaystyle|{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{3}|

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}p\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\left(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\right)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|Du|\,|u|\,|\nabla\zeta|\zeta^{p-1}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}p\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\left(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\right)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}|u|\,|\nabla\zeta|\zeta^{p-1}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

We apply Young’s inequality with exponents
p
,
p^{\prime}
, and a parameter
\varepsilon>0
to a part of the integrand on the right-hand side to obtain

\displaystyle C_{1}p\zeta^{p-1}\left(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\right)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}|u|\,|\nabla\zeta|

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\,\zeta^{p}\left(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}+C(p)C_{1}^{p}\varepsilon^{1-p}|u|^{p}|\nabla\zeta|^{p}.

Substituting this into the previous estimate, we obtain

\displaystyle|{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{3}|

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\big(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\big)_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+C(p)C_{1}^{p}\varepsilon^{1-p}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|u|^{p}\big(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\big)_{+}^{p^{\prime}}|\nabla\zeta|^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

It remains to control the terms that arise due to the source terms
{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}
and
{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}
. We begin with the contribution from the divergence term. We write

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\big[{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{1}+{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{2}+{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{3}\big]\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

with the abbreviations

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p-2}{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}\cdot D_{\alpha}u\,(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p},

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{2}

\displaystyle:=p\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p-2}{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}\cdot u\,(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p-1}D_{\alpha}\zeta,

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{3}

\displaystyle:=p^{\prime}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p-2}{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}_{\alpha}\cdot u\,\frac{D_{\alpha}u\cdot u}{|u|}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}-1}\zeta^{p}.

The terms
{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{1}
and
{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{2}
can easily be estimated.
In fact,
applying Young’s inequality to the term
{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{1}
gives

\displaystyle|{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{1}|

\displaystyle\leq|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p-1}\,|Du|(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon|Du|^{p}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}+\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2})^{\tfrac{p}{2}}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}+\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}.

A similar application of Young’s inequality to
{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{2}
yields

\displaystyle|{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{2}|

\displaystyle\leq p|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p-1}\zeta^{p-1}|u|\,|\nabla\zeta|(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon|u|^{p}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}|\nabla\zeta|^{p}+C(p)\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}.

We now consider
{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{3}
. By Young’s inequality,

\displaystyle|{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{3}|

\displaystyle\leq p^{\prime}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p-1}|u||Du|(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}-1}\zeta^{p}

\displaystyle=\Big(|Du|(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}-1}\zeta\Big)\Big(p^{\prime}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p-1}|u|\mathbf{1}_{\{|u|>{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\}}\zeta^{p-1}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon|Du|^{p}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}+C(p)\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p}|u|^{p^{\prime}}\mathbf{1}_{\{|u|>{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\}}\zeta^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2})^{\tfrac{p}{2}}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}+C(p)\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p}|u|^{p^{\prime}}\mathbf{1}_{\{|u|>{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\}}\zeta^{p}.

Using the three preceding estimates in
{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}
, that is, multiplying by
\chi
and integrating over
Q_{R,S}
, we obtain

\displaystyle|{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}|

\displaystyle\leq 2\varepsilon\iint_{Q_{R,S}}(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2})^{\tfrac{p}{2}}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\varepsilon\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|u|^{p}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}|\nabla\zeta|^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+C(p)\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p}|u|^{p^{\prime}}\mathbf{1}_{\{|u|>{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\}}\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

It remains to estimate the term
|{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}|
. We have

\displaystyle|{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}|

\displaystyle\leq\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}||u|(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}||u|^{p^{\prime}+1}\mathbf{1}_{\{|u|>{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\}}\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

since
(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\leq|u|^{p^{\prime}}\mathbf{1}_{\{|u|>{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\}}
.
Combining the bounds for for
{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{3}
,
{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}
, and
{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}
,
we obtain

\displaystyle|{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{3}|+|{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}|+|{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}|

\displaystyle\leq 3\varepsilon\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\big(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\big(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\big)_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\big(C(p)C_{1}^{p}\varepsilon^{1-p}+\varepsilon\big)\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|u|^{p}\big(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\big)_{+}^{p^{\prime}}|\nabla\zeta|^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+C(p)\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p}|u|^{p^{\prime}}\mathbf{1}_{\{|u|>{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\}}\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}||u|^{p^{\prime}+1}\mathbf{1}_{\{|u|>{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\}}\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

We use this to estimate the right-hand side of (
3.4
), together with the lower bound for
{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{1}+{\bm{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}
. In addition, we absorb the
3\varepsilon
-term into the left-hand side. In the time term we also split the derivative of
\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}
. In this way we obtain

\displaystyle-\iint_{Q_{R,S}}

\displaystyle v\zeta^{p}\partial_{t}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\big(C_{o}-3\varepsilon\big)\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\left(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\big(C(p)C_{1}^{p}\varepsilon^{1-p}+\varepsilon\big)\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|u|^{p}\big(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\big)_{+}^{p^{\prime}}|\nabla\zeta|^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+C_{o}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\mu^{p}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+C(p)\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p}|u|^{p^{\prime}}\mathbf{1}_{\{|u|>{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\}}\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}||u|^{p^{\prime}+1}\mathbf{1}_{\{|u|>{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\}}\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\iint_{Q_{R,S}}v\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\partial_{t}\zeta^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

At this stage we fix

\varepsilon:=\tfrac{1}{6}C_{o}
.
Then
C_{o}-3\varepsilon=\tfrac{1}{2}C_{o}
, and the previous inequality becomes

\displaystyle-\iint_{Q_{R,S}}

\displaystyle v\zeta^{p}\partial_{t}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\tfrac{1}{2}C_{o}\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\left(\mu^{2}+|Du|^{2}\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\iint_{Q_{R,S}}\big[|u|^{p}|\nabla\zeta|^{p}+\mu^{p}\zeta^{p}\big]\big(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\big)_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+C\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p}|u|^{p^{\prime}}\mathbf{1}_{\{|u|>{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\}}\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\iint_{Q_{R,S}}|{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}||u|^{p^{\prime}+1}\mathbf{1}_{\{|u|>{\bm{\mathsf{k}}}\}}\zeta^{p}\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\iint_{Q_{R,S}}v\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\partial_{t}\zeta^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

for a constant
C=C(p,C_{o}/C_{1})
. In particular, one may take
C\simeq C(p)C_{o}\,(C_{1}/C_{o})^{p^{\prime}}
. Here
p^{\prime}=\tfrac{p}{p-1}\geq 2
, and we have used
C_{1}\geq 1
to simplify the constants. Arrived at this point, we let
\delta\downarrow 0
in the preceding inequality. In the limit we have
\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}\downarrow\bm{\hbox{\raise 1.94444pt\hbox{$\chi$}}}_{(-S,\tau]}
. The first integral on the left-hand side converges to

\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{B_{R}\times\{\tau\}}v\zeta^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x,

while the second integral is bounded below by

\iint_{B_{R}\times(-S,\tau]}|\nabla|u||^{p}(|u|-{\bm{\mathsf{k}}})_{+}^{p^{\prime}}\zeta^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.

On the right-hand side we simply bound
\chi
by
1
. In the obtained inequality we take the supremum over
\tau\in(-S,0)
in the first term, and let
\tau\uparrow 0
in the second. Hence we obtain the desired energy estimate.
∎

3.2.
Moser’s iteration: from
L^{{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}}
to
L^{\infty}

Building on the local energy estimate from the previous
subsection, one can apply the Moser iteration scheme to upgrade an
a
priori

L^{r}
-integrability assumption to an
L^{\infty}
-bound. The
mechanism is the familiar nonlinear iteration on shrinking cylinders,
whose effectiveness relies on the positivity of the parameter

\bm{\lambda}_{r}
. The following lemma provides the resulting
estimate for weak solutions to (
1.1
).

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
p\in(1,\frac{2N}{N+2}]
,
q>\frac{N+p}{p}
,
{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}\in L^{qp}_{\rm loc}\big(E_{T},\mathbb{R}^{kN}\big)
,
{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}\in L^{qp^{\prime}}_{\rm loc}\big(E_{T},\mathbb{R}^{k}\big)
, and

{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}>2
such that

\bm{\lambda}_{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}:=N(p-2)+p{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}>0.

Then any local, weak solution
u

to (
1.1
) in
E_{T}
with (
1.2
)
satisfying

u\in L^{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}_{\rm loc}(E_{T},\mathbb{R}^{k}),

is locally bounded in
E_{T}
.

Remark 3.4
.

It is possible to extract an explicit bound of
u
out of Moser’s iteration. See
[
2
, Proposition 4.1]
in this connection. However, such form of bound is insufficient for our later application.

Proof..

The proof proceeds in two steps. In the first step, towards establishing the local
L^{\infty}
-bound for
u
, we refine the energy inequality from Lemma
3.1
under the additional assumption that
|u|\in L_{\rm loc}^{2+2\alpha}(E_{T})
for some
\alpha\geq 0
. This is achieved by deriving a quantitative reverse Hölder inequality for
|u|
. In the second step, we apply an iteration scheme to this reverse Hölder inequality. Starting from the assumption
u\in L^{\bm{\mathsf{r}}}_{\rm loc}(E_{T})
, we gradually improve the integrability of
|u|
, while carefully controlling the constants that arise at each stage. Passing to the limit in this iteration ultimately yields the desired local
L^{\infty}
-bound. Throughout the proof, we always assume that
\varrho\leq 1
.

Step 1.

Fix
\alpha\geq 0
and a cylinder
Q_{\varrho}(z_{o})\equiv B_{\varrho}(x_{o})\times(t_{o}-\varrho^{p},t_{o}]\Subset E_{T}
.
Assume in addition that

|u|\in L^{2+2\alpha}_{\rm loc}(E_{T}).

By translation, we can always assume that
z_{o}=(0,0)
. In the energy inequality (
3.1
) we employ the testing function

\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}(s)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}k^{2\alpha},&\mbox{if $0\leq s\leq k^{2}$,}\\[5.0pt]
s^{\alpha},&\mbox{if $k^{2}<s<\ell^{2}$,}\\[5.0pt]
\ell^{2\alpha},&\mbox{if $s\geq\ell^{2}$,}\end{array}\right.

and consider the auxiliary function

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{|u|^{2}}\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s.

The function
\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}
is identical to
\phi_{\alpha,k,\ell}
defined in
[
2
, Eqn. (4.2)]
taking
m=1
there.
By the same consideration as in
[
2
]
, one checks the following algebraic estimates of
v
, namely

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{1+\alpha}\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)^{\frac{1+\alpha}{\alpha}}-1\leq v\leq|u|^{2+2\alpha}+1.

In addition, by the chain rule for Sobolev functions, also Kato’s inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\Big|\nabla\big[\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)\big]^{\frac{p+2\alpha}{2p\alpha}}\Big|^{p}

\displaystyle\qquad=\Big|\tfrac{p+2\alpha}{2p\alpha}\big[\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)\big]^{\frac{p+2\alpha}{2p\alpha}-1}\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}^{\prime}\big(|u|^{2}\big)\nabla|u|^{2}\Big|^{p}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\big(\tfrac{p+2\alpha}{p\alpha}\big)^{p}\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)|\nabla|u||^{p}\Big[\big[\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)\big]^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}-1}\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}^{\prime}\big(|u|^{2}\big)|u|\Big]^{p}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\big(\tfrac{p+2\alpha}{p\alpha}\big)^{p}\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)|Du|^{p}\Big[\big[\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)\big]^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}-1}\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}^{\prime}\big(|u|^{2}\big)|u|\Big]^{p}.

If
k<|u|<\ell
, then

\displaystyle\big[\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)\big]^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}-1}\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}^{\prime}\big(|u|^{2}\big)|u|

\displaystyle=\alpha|u|^{2\alpha(\frac{1}{2\alpha}-1)}|u|^{2(\alpha-1)}|u|=\alpha.

For
|u|>\ell
or
|u|<k
we have

\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}^{\prime}\big(|u|^{2})=0
.
In either case it follows that

\displaystyle\Big|\nabla\big[\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)\big]^{\frac{p+2\alpha}{2p\alpha}}\Big|^{p}

\displaystyle\leq(p+2\alpha)^{p}|Du|^{p}\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big).

Finally, we note that

\displaystyle\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)+|u|^{2}\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}^{\prime}\big(|u|^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\leq(1+\alpha)\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big).

Inserting the preparatory bounds into (
3.1
) yields

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{1+\alpha}\sup_{\tau\in(-\varrho^{p},0]}

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\varrho}\times\{\tau\}}\zeta^{p}\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)^{\frac{1+\alpha}{\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+\tfrac{C_{o}}{(p+2\alpha)^{p}}\iint_{Q_{\varrho}}\zeta^{p}\Big|\nabla\big[\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)\big]^{\frac{p+2\alpha}{2p\alpha}}\Big|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\iint_{Q_{\varrho}}\big[|u|^{p}|\nabla\zeta|^{p}+\mu^{p}\zeta^{p}\big]\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+C\iint_{Q_{\varrho}}\big[|u|^{2+2\alpha}+1\big]|\partial_{t}\zeta^{p}|\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\sup_{\tau\in(-\varrho^{p},0]}\int_{B_{\varrho}\times\{\tau\}}\zeta^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+C\Big(1+\alpha^{\frac{p}{p-1}}\Big)\iint_{Q_{\varrho}}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p}\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)\zeta^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+C\iint_{Q_{\varrho}}|{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}||u|\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)\zeta^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

where
C=C(p,C_{o},C_{1})
.
On the right-hand side, the first term is estimated by using
\mu^{p}\zeta^{p}\leq 1
and

\big(1+|u|^{p}\big)\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)\leq 2\big(|u|^{2+2\alpha}+1\big).

Hence, together with the second term they are bounded by

C(1+\|\nabla\zeta\|^{p}_{\infty}+\|\partial_{t}\zeta\|_{\infty})\iint_{Q_{\varrho}}\big(|u|^{2+2\alpha}+1\big)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

whereas the third term is simply bounded by
1
using
\varrho\leq 1
. The forth term is estimated by Hölder’s inequality with exponents
q
and
\frac{q}{q-1}
:

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{\varrho}}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{p}\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)\zeta^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\bigg[\iint_{Q_{\varrho}}|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}|^{qp}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{1}{q}}{\bm{\mathsf{T}}},

where we defined

{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}:=\bigg[\iint_{Q_{\varrho}}\big(\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)\zeta^{p}\big)^{\frac{q}{q-1}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\Big]^{\frac{q-1}{q}}.

The fifth term is again estimated by using Hölder’s inequality, now with exponents

qp^{\prime}
,
p
, and
\frac{qp^{\prime}}{q-1}
, followed by Young’s inequality with exponents
p
and
p^{\prime}
. This gives

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{\varrho}}|{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}||u|\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)\zeta^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad=\iint_{Q_{\varrho}}|{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}|\big[|u|\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\zeta\big]\big[\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)\zeta^{p}\big]^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad\leq\bigg[\iint_{Q_{\varrho}}|u|^{p}\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)\zeta^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad\cdot\bigg[\iint_{Q_{\varrho}}|{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}|^{qp^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{1}{qp^{\prime}}}\bigg[\iint_{Q_{\varrho}}\big(\zeta^{p}\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)\big)^{\frac{q}{q-1}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{q-1}{qp^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\varrho^{-p}\iint_{Q_{\varrho}}|u|^{p}\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)\zeta^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\bigg[\iint_{Q_{\varrho}}|{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}|^{qp^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{1}{q}}{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}.

The integrals of
{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}
and
{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}
can be extended to
Q_{1}
without loss of generality. Their contributions will not be traced in what follows but simply absorbed into a lump constant
C
.

On the left-hand side of the energy estimate, we absorb the cut-off function
\zeta

into the gradient in the second integral. For this we use the following estimate

\displaystyle\Big|\nabla\big[\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}

\displaystyle\big(|u|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p+2\alpha}{2p\alpha}}\zeta\big]\Big|^{p}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}\Big|\nabla\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p+2\alpha}{2p\alpha}}\Big|^{p}\zeta^{p}+2^{p-1}\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p+2\alpha}{2\alpha}}|\nabla\zeta|^{p}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}\Big|\nabla\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p+2\alpha}{2\alpha}}\Big|^{p}\zeta^{p}+2^{p-1}\big(|u|^{p}+1\big)\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)|\nabla\zeta|^{p}.

Collecting all the preceding estimates, absorbing the cut-off function into the gradient and shifting the coefficients containing
\alpha
to the right-hand side, we arrive at

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau\in[-\varrho^{p},0]}

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\varrho}\times\{\tau\}}\zeta^{p}\,\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)^{\frac{1+\alpha}{\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}x+\iint_{Q_{\varrho}}\Big|\nabla\big[\zeta\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p+2\alpha}{2p\alpha}}\big]\Big|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\,(1+\alpha)^{p}(\varrho^{-p}+\|\nabla\zeta\|^{p}_{\infty}+\|\partial_{t}\zeta\|_{\infty})\iint_{Q_{\varrho}}\big[|u|^{2+2\alpha}+1\big]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\,}+C(1+\alpha)^{p^{\prime}+p}{\bm{\mathsf{T}}},

From now on
C
also depends on
\|{\bm{\mathsf{f}}}\|_{qp^{\prime},Q_{1}}
and
\|{\bm{\mathsf{F}}}\|_{qp,Q_{1}}
. The left-hand side will be denoted as
{\bm{\mathsf{L}}}
for short. In what follows we aim to show
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}\leq\varepsilon{\bm{\mathsf{L}}}+\text{[other terms]}
and absorb
{\bm{\mathsf{L}}}
to the left in the energy estimate.
To this end, we denote

(3.5)

{\bm{\mathsf{m}}}=p\frac{2+2\alpha}{p+2\alpha},\qquad{\bm{\mathsf{q}}}=p\frac{N+{\bm{\mathsf{m}}}}{N},

invoke the parabolic embedding (Proposition 4.1 in the Preliminaries of
[
6
]
) with

(v,p,m,q)=\Big(\zeta\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p+2\alpha}{2p\alpha}},p,{\bm{\mathsf{m}}},{\bm{\mathsf{q}}}\Big),

and obtain that

(3.6)

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{\varrho}}

\displaystyle\Big(\zeta\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p+2\alpha}{2p\alpha}}\Big)^{{\bm{\mathsf{q}}}}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\iint_{Q_{\varrho}}\Big|\nabla\Big[\zeta\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p+2\alpha}{2p\alpha}}\Big]\Big|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cdot\bigg[\sup_{\tau\in(-\varrho^{p},0]}\int_{B_{\varrho}\times\{\tau\}}\Big[\zeta\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p+2\alpha}{2p\alpha}}\Big]^{p\frac{2+2\alpha}{p+2\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg]^{\frac{p}{N}}

\displaystyle\leq C\iint_{Q_{\varrho}}\Big|\nabla\Big[\zeta\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p+2\alpha}{2p\alpha}}\Big]\Big|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cdot\bigg[\sup_{\tau\in(-\varrho^{p},0]}\int_{B_{\varrho}\times\{\tau\}}\zeta^{p}\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)^{\frac{1+\alpha}{\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg]^{\frac{p}{N}}

\displaystyle=C\underbrace{\iint_{Q_{\varrho}}\Big|\nabla\Big[\zeta\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)^{\frac{p+2\alpha}{2p\alpha}}\Big]\Big|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t}_{\leq{\bm{\mathsf{L}}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cdot\bigg[\underbrace{\sup_{\tau\in(-\varrho^{p},0]}\int_{B_{\varrho}\times\{\tau\}}\zeta^{p}\Phi_{\alpha,\ell,k}\big(|u|^{2}\big)^{\frac{1+\alpha}{\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}x}_{\leq{\bm{\mathsf{L}}}}\bigg]^{\frac{p}{N}}

\displaystyle\leq C{\bm{\mathsf{L}}}^{\frac{N+p}{N}}.

Noticing that
\frac{q}{q-1}<\frac{N+p}{N}<{\bm{\mathsf{q}}}\frac{p+2\alpha}{2p\alpha}
, we estimate
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
by first applying
the interpolation inequality of the form
